# Architecture and plan

## Goal

Patch a small modular synth in the browser, run it on an M-VAVE FM-1. No FM engine, sampler, sequencer or UI from Felucca: only its flashing path and its hardware layer.

## What the FM-1 is (as far as known)

From the Felucca and FM-1 reverse-engineering projects: a JieLi AC791N SoC (pi32v2 core), 240×240 colour LCD, 27 keys, 7 knobs plus volume, USB MIDI/audio, TRS MIDI in, battery. CPU clock, RAM and flash sizes are **not verified** here; Felucca's `firmware/app.ld` and `tools/build.py` hold the memory map (app slot 0x8DFBC bytes, app RAM + a pool region).

## Flash path (reused from Felucca)

1. Browser (or `tools/fm1_install.py`) talks SysEx over USB MIDI. The *device* asks for blocks of a "logical image" (the package minus 20 marker bytes) and the host answers.
2. Step 1: the running firmware verifies parts of the package and stages Felucca's update loader. Step 2: the loader reads the whole package and writes flash. Then the device restarts and the identity (`FM-1_<n>`; ours `FM-1_7MMmmpp`) is checked.
3. A package is built by `tools/fm1pkg_make.py` / `tools/build.py` (app.bin + loader ota.bin + three JieLi SDK files). `buildFeluccaPackage` (`web/src/flasher/fm1pkg.js`) can patch an app.bin into the user's own official V15; the Flash view does not offer it (#161).

## Patch engine

`web/src/engine.ts` and `web/src/modules.ts` are the reference implementation: float DSP, 128-frame blocks, patch graph ordered by `processingOrder` (loops become one-block feedback). Module definitions (`ModuleDef`) are data and are shared by the editor and, later, the firmware.

**Sample rate.** The FM-1's I2S runs at 44118 Hz (M-VAVE's own board code, `fm1_open` `app/board/fm1_audio.h`, `FM1_AUDIO_HARDWARE_RATE`; nominally 11.2896 MHz / 256), so `ENG_SR` (engine.h) and `DEVICE_SR` (modules.ts) are 44118 since 1.1; before that the firmware assumed 44100 and played 0.7 cent sharp with times 0.04 % short. A 128-frame half buffer is 2901 us. The golden test renders the reference at `DEVICE_SR`. The browser keeps its AudioContext's own rate (48 or 44.1 kHz): the engine works in Hz and seconds at any rate, so it already sounds as the device does; a 44118 Hz context would only add a resampler to the output. The delay and reverb lines are fixed sample counts at `DEVICE_SR` (the firmware's arrays) and scale with the rate in the browser. USB audio out (usb.c, `FELUCCA_UAC`, on since 2026-10-21; checked on a Mac 2026-10-21 and on an iPad 2026-11-02, below): the FM-1 is a UAC1 recording device, 16-bit stereo at 44.1 kHz only (48 kHz needs Felucca's resampler table `uac_fir.h`, not in this repo: `FELUCCA_UAC_48K` 0, the format descriptor lists 44100 alone, SET_CUR is acknowledged and changes nothing). The isochronous endpoint is asynchronous: packets of 44 / 45 frames, one more or one fewer while the ring's fill at a render's start is outside 160..208 frames, absorb the 18 extra frames a second (about 18 longer packets a second). audio.c taps each half's I2S words >> 8 after the render; The interfaces come in this order: 0 audio control, 1 audio streaming (EP 0x84), 2 MIDI streaming (EP 0x01 / 0x81); the audio control header lists {1, 2}, and bcdDevice is 3.90 (`FELUCCA_UAC_MIDI_LAST`, [D-478](ledger/link-and-midi.md#D-478)). With MIDI as interface 1, as up to 1.8.2, iPadOS and macOS 15 show no audio input, and the iPad no MIDI port. Update mode presents the MIDI-only descriptors of a build without it (`usb_uac_switch`), the update loader never had it; `tools/usb_test.sh` pins both layouts' bytes. Diagnostics: `m1_link.py uac`; host test `tools/usb_test.sh`. The USB setup log (#535, `usb.c` `ul_ring`): every control transfer a host starts (its 8 SETUP bytes, the time, and what the firmware answered: data sent, acknowledged, stalled, unknown, aborted by the host, status stage done) and each bus event (reset, suspend, resume, the address taking effect, the USB controller (its serial interface engine) starting or going silent) go into a ring of 64 entries of 16 bytes in `.lowram` (1,092 B with its counters, [D-485](ledger/link-and-midi.md#D-485)). Nothing clears it but a power cut, so a host that fails can be read from another: `m1_link.py usblog` decodes DIAG pages 40..71 (the ring), 104 (its state) and 105 (its counters); `usblog arm` (page 106) keeps the next 64 entries instead of the newest, `usblog wrap` (page 107) goes back. It only watches: `tools/usb_test.sh` checks that an enumeration makes the same register writes and packets with it and without it (`USB_LOG=0`, as the update loader builds). Since #535 `usb_start` writes the controller's POWER register as 0x40 (SoftConn), not 0x60: in the usual register map of this kind of controller (Mentor MUSB) the extra bit lets it negotiate high speed, and every descriptor is a full-speed one (USB 1.10). That this controller follows that map is not confirmed: POWER reads 0x70 after a reset with either value, and macOS reports the FM-1 at full speed with both (ioreg "Device Speed" 1). `FELUCCA_USB_HS=1` builds the old value; the update loader keeps it, so its binary stays as shipped ([D-479](ledger/link-and-midi.md#D-479)). **The iPad** (the owner's test, 2026-11-02, iPadOS 26, this build): through a USB hub plugged into the iPad and not into a wall socket, and a USB-C cable from a hub port to the FM-1, the iPad set the FM-1 up; MIDI Wrench saw its MIDI port and its MIDI, and Reel recorded from it (two takes, 30 s and 68 s, no resets, no underruns). The iPad's battery icon never showed charging, so the FM-1 does not need power from the hub's own supply. Apps that want 48 kHz (Voice Memos, Tape16) do not list it. Earlier tries with the interface order of [D-478](ledger/link-and-midi.md#D-478) and POWER 0x60: a USB-C to USB-C cable gave nothing (no bus reset in the setup log: the FM-1's USB-C port does not signal a device on its configuration channel (CC) pins, so the iPad never powers the connection); a USB-C to USB-A adapter and an A-to-C cable gave 11,269 bus resets in 49 s and no request. Whether POWER 0x40 or the hub made the difference was not separated. Use a hub.

## Firmware

Builds with `tools/build.py` (in CI: `.github/workflows/firmware.yml`; see BUILDING.md), flashes with the web flasher or `tools/fm1_install.py`.

- `firmware/src/engine.c` is the fixed-point port of `web/src/engine.ts` and `modules.ts` (all 13 modules, mono voice, CV inputs, feedback cables as one block of delay). Signals and parameters are Q16.16, oscillator phase u32, filter coefficients Q24, envelope Q30; sine, exp2 and tan come from tables (`tools/gen_dsp_tables.py` -> `dsp_tables.h`). No float anywhere. The 32x32->64 multiply and the 32-bit divide are hardware; 64-bit division (a libcall) is used only at block rate and on waveform edges.
- `firmware/src/defs.h` and `presets.h` are generated from the TypeScript definitions (`npm run export-defs` in `web/`): module/port/parameter indices and the factory patches in the binary format of `web/src/patchbin.ts`. Port and type indices are append-only.
- The audio ISR (`audio.c`) renders 128 frames per half buffer. MIDI program change n loads bank slot n, note on/off and CC 123 drive the voice. The LCD shows the patch name in the top bar; the measured CPU load (ISR time per half buffer, key scan and USB nested in it) is kept in `eng_us_last` / `eng_us_max` but no longer drawn.
- `tools/golden.sh` renders 11 patches with the TypeScript engine and the C engine (host build) and compares them. Smooth patches agree to about 0.1 % rms (-60 dB), patches with saw/square edges to 1-2 % rms: the Q16 pitch grid moves an edge by a fraction of a sample, which gives short spikes at the edges and no audible difference.
- Memory (one build since #59, 2026-10-18: the four-voice build is gone; one arena since #649, [D-381](ledger/engine.md#D-381), Proposed): one engine and one arena per patch, `eng_arena` in the pool (344,628 B, `ENG_ARENA`: as big as the fixed pools it replaced, 128 instances of 264 B, 255 signal buffers of 512 B, 1536 parameter values of 4 B and the 167,980 B line area, plus the spare copy, and since #721 the 64 modules of 92 B (`node_t`, `ENG_NODE_BYTES`) the fixed module table held). A load divides it: from the bottom the spare copy (`inst_pool[0]`, every node's until its own are taken) and each module's copies (`arena_inst`, taken with its record), then the signal buffers (`buf_alloc`); from the top the patch's modules (`arena_nodes`, `E.node`: 92 B each, #721, so a patch pays for its own modules and one of up to 64 has the room it had), then the parameter values (`par_take`: each module its type's parameters, twice that with a morph B side, and each copy its type's effective values, not `MAX_PAR` each, [D-369](ledger/engine.md#D-369)); the lines last, into what is left (`arena_free`, counted in the FM-1's bytes so a 64-bit host build loads the same patches). A patch that does not fit does not load (-4 lines, -6 buffers, -7 copies, -8 values). The counts are sanity caps: 254 copies (`ENG_MAX_INST`: `inst0` is a byte), 1023 buffers (`ENG_NBUF`), 128 lines (`ENG_MAX_LINES`, eng_load's lists on the stack). The editor's budget counts the same (`poolUse`, `lineBudget`, the MEMORY line with what takes most; golden kits check the copies, buffers and values against eng_load's, `host_test` with the kit's `.par`). The **effect memory** ([D-375](ledger/engine.md#D-375)): the lines of every Delay, reverb, chorus, string, comb and Granular in two areas, what the arena has left and `line_low` in `.lowram` (26,196 B: what the reverb and chorus lines took and 8 KiB more), divided at load by `line_place` (the largest line first, each into the area with the least room that holds it; the editor's `linePlace` / `lineBudget` model it). The lines: a Delay's from its `length` (`dly_samples`: 2 x ceil(length x 44118 / 2000) + 60 samples, 700 ms = 30944 samples, 61,888 B), a string or comb line per copy 2,208 B, a granular line 88,428 B, a reverb 16,148 B, a chorus 1,856 B; no count limits them, the 1.0 release build (release.yml run 39685196079, a25c1bf): image 242,056 B; RAM .data+.bss 16,416 of 24,576 B (8,160 B free); pool 377,544 of 417,792 B (40,248 B free; `tools/pool_sizes.sh`'s stand-in for the same commit: 377,542 B). Limits: 128 modules (`ENG_MAX_MODULES`, engine.h, #721; 64 before 1.11: a patch with more is written in format 51), 255 cables, 160 summed inputs, 320 sources, patches up to 3840 bytes (a bank slot is one 4 KB sector with a 256 B header); the HELLO reply says 128 / 255 / 1. A set of modules (the armed set, what arming a device arms) is `eng_mset_t`, four u32 words (`ENG_MSET_WORDS`); the link's ARMS / ARMS_NOW carry the four words with INFO feature bit 21, the first two to and from older firmware and editors.
- **Memory map since #273** (`firmware/app.ld`, [D-373](ledger/engine.md#D-373)): `.ram_text` (the flash driver, 3.6 KB with the store's stepped writes of #656; the app leaves out `fl_status_ram`, which it never calls, and the update loader keeps it, `FL_STATUS_RAM`, #725) and after it `.lowram`, the SRAM up to 0x01C07F00 that Felucca kept for a RAM-run debug build (29.5 KB with the rest of the old 24 KB code window; sloop-fm1 found and device-checked it). `.lowram` holds `line_low`, the effect memory's second line area (26,196 B, [D-375](ledger/engine.md#D-375); until 1.10 the reverb and chorus lines, 18,004 B), and the USB setup log (1,092 B, #535, [D-485](ledger/link-and-midi.md#D-485)); the rest, 1,484 B, is free: room for more RAM code (#725); the second core's waiting loop (`.c1_text`, 160 B, #271) sits after `.ram_text`, and its stacks are not here (below). The boot writes two patterns into every word of it, reads them back and zeroes it (`main.c lowram_prove`). The stacks: main 20,224 B, supervisor 7,936 B (each with a 256 B guard band below it); below the main stack's guard band, 4 KiB for the second core's stacks (#271, [D-379](ledger/engine.md#D-379): user 3,584 B, exceptions 512 B), which the main stack gave up (it was 24,320 B). The boot paints both stacks below its own frame (`stack_paint`, the word 0x5AC3A55C); `tools/m1_link.py mem` (DIAG page 124) reads the proof and how deep each stack has gone since boot. On a test FM-1 (2026-10-24, a canary build that only watched the RAM below the RAM region: none of its words changed through an install, 26 device scenarios, 10 min of every bank slot with chords, 10 min of editor link traffic, 5 min of USB audio recording, 5 min of MIDI clock with program changes and a firmware update over itself) the main stack held at most 2,764 B and the supervisor stack 2,052 B. In the emulator (fm1-emu, which paints the area itself before boot so older builds can be measured too) the main stack held at most 2,508 B over `emu:smoke`. The build (CI): pool 378,916 of 417,792 B (38,876 B free, 30,684 above the 8 KB the build keeps spare); `.lowram` 20,120 B (9,468 B free). Moving the reverb and chorus lines freed 18,004 B of pool; the delay line took 15,888 B more (46,000 -> 61,888 B, 521 -> 700 ms).

Measured on the FM-1: the 303 Acid factory patch (8 modules, one delay) uses 11 % of the audio budget (peak 12 %).

Patch transfer (M1-0.3): `web/src/protocol.ts` defines the SysEx messages (`F0 7D 4D 31 cmd payload F7`, payload packed to 7 bit): HELLO/INFO, patch upload in 384-byte CHUNKs with an ACK each, COMMIT with a CRC-16, STORE, live PARAM. `firmware/src/proto.c` implements it (host-tested bit-exactly by `firmware/test/proto_test.c` through `tools/golden.sh`); for where patches are stored see [D-257](ledger/patch-format.md#D-257). A failed commit leaves the previous patch running; STORE saves the live-edited parameters too. `web/src/device.ts` is the browser side (toolbar: Connect FM-1, Live, Send patch, Store on FM-1); `tools/m1_link.py` does the same from a terminal. On the FM-1 one received SysEx frame waits at a time (`usb.c`; the host is held off until the main loop releases it), and the main loop looks at it once: the editor's handler, else the update protocol's (`firmware/src/host_frame.h`, host test `tools/frame_test.sh`; #486). DIAG pages 118 (in) and 117 (out) count the link's frames since boot: frames finished, cut short or not taken, which handler took each, messages sent and sends refused (`web/scripts/rig-peak.mjs --link` sets them beside what the rig's page sent and got). Connect finds the FM-1 by HELLO, not by port name ([D-474](ledger/link-and-midi.md#D-474), #187): `linkCandidates` pairs inputs and outputs of one name, those named like an FM-1 first (three tries each), then every other pair (one try); macOS keeps the name it first saw for a USB id at a socket (the rig's Matryoshka shows as "Felucca", `~/Library/Audio/MIDI Configurations/Default.mcfg`). `notFound` lists the ports seen, `sysexRefused` says where to allow SysEx; the panel follower then listens on the link's input (`DeviceLink.input`).

**Parameter inputs and the source matrix (patch format 2, M1-0.6).** Every module except the FM-1 panel and the output has exactly two parameter inputs, `p1` and `p2`, each with an attenuverter (-2..2 since 1.9, [D-293](ledger/patch-format.md#D-293); scales and inverts), and a matrix with the sources p1 and p2 on one axis and the module's parameters on the other (the knob columns k1..k4 are gone, [D-1001](ledger/patch-format.md#D-1001)). A parameter's effective value is base + (block mean of p1 x att1 and of p2 x att2, for the inputs the matrix assigns to it) x (max - min), clamped. On the FM-1 the base a knob's click sets (K1..K4, or MIDI CC 16..19) reaches the module over `ENG_RAMP_BLOCKS` = 8 blocks of 128 frames (23 ms; #405, [D-374](ledger/engine.md#D-374)): `eng_knob_param` gives the parameter its value at once (the editor's mirror, MIDI out, a save and the screen see it) and `apply_cv` works from a base the knob's pending ramp still offsets, shrinking by one step a block (`ramp_start`, `ramp_tick`, `ramp_base`); a choice row, a poly row, a setting, a patch load, a preset, the editor's sends and the morph set at once, and the browser engine does not ramp. On the FM-1 the knobs K1..K4 edit the rows of the device page in focus (since format 29; one click = 1/100 of the range, or the parameter's own step if larger; five times that when turned fast, but only after a click the same way within 80 ms, a reversal being one click; HOME held, a tenth of a click and never under the parameter's step, `defs.h` `pstep`, a logarithmic row 1/1000 octave; #340, [D-244](ledger/device-ui.md#D-244)). The FM-1 panel module is the patch's only input and has no matrix (there is no separate MIDI module: the keys and USB MIDI are handled by the firmware, and by the browser host, and play the one voice that the panel module outputs as pitch, gate and vel; a saved `midi` module becomes the panel module on load). Default matrix: p1 -> parameter 1, p2 -> parameter 2. Old (version 1) JSON patches with `<param>_cv` cables are converted on load. The editor keeps each card's two inputs and exposes input aliases on parameter slider thumbs. Per-parameter routing menus replace the checkbox grid, including attenuverter depth and macro ranges. `ParamDef.input` identifies dedicated CV aliases; other thumb drops reuse or allocate p1/p2 through `parameter-routing.ts`. New sources never silently enter pins shared by unrelated parameters. The persisted matrix remains compatible; routing changes re-send the patch, while attenuverters, ranges and parameters are live.

**Preset bank (M1-0.8).** The firmware has the factory patches compiled in (`presets.h`, generated from `web/presets/*.json` through `web/src/presets.ts`; 29, see "Factory patches"). The editor's Library > Patches keeps the bank's offline copy (up to 64 patches: factory ones and the user's), orders it and sends it to the FM-1, or with a connected FM-1 writes, erases and lists its slots directly ("The Library" in the Emulator view's section below): each patch goes into one slot (`slotstore.c`, 64 slots over 66 flash sectors, header with name and CRC written last; see [D-257](ledger/patch-format.md#D-257) and "The bank" below) through the SLOT / SLOT_ERASE / SLOT_INFO commands, without being loaded. The PRESET knob (encoder 6) and MIDI program change step through the occupied slots; the top bar shows the patch name.

See [D-203](ledger/device-ui.md#D-203).

**Releasing (#164).** A release package is built by hand in CI: `gh workflow run release.yml -f version=1.8.0` (or `1.8.0-rc1` for a dry run) runs `tools/build.py --release 1.8.0` (identity `FM-1_7010800`: a 7, then major, minor and patch as two digits each, [D-178](ledger/firmware-install.md#D-178); version `v1.8.0`; the patch is required and each number runs 0..99; `build.py` checks that the identity fits the package's 20 marker characters and the handshake's 27 bytes and that no marker byte encodes to 0x7D), the same no-warnings and pool checks and host tests as firmware.yml, and uploads `matryoshka-release-1.8.0` = `release-1.8.0/` (package, its `.json` sidecar, the app `.bin`, `SHA256SUMS`, licences, build log), and `matryoshka-emu-midi`, the `M1_EMU_MIDI=1` build of the same sources for the boot check (`npm run device:scenarios -- --install <id>` installs the release run's package on the test rig after it, issue #783); the run summary has the image / RAM / pool line and the checksums. Dev and CI builds report `FM-1_7000000`; releases 1.0..1.7 report `FM-1_7XY` (one digit each for major and minor), and every host parser takes both forms (`fm1pkg.js` `matryoshkaVersion`, `fm1_install.py` `matryoshka_version`). The firmware also sends its major, minor and patch as INFO's last three bytes (0.0.0 for dev and CI builds); on connect the editor compares them (or, for firmware before 1.8, the identity's) with the bundled package's version (`fm1-version.ts`) and offers Update when the FM-1's is older. Then: `gh run download <id> -n matryoshka-release-1.8.0 -D build/release-1.8.0`; `npm run emu:smoke -- build/release-1.8.0/matryoshka-1.8.0-app.bin` on that exact build, unless its `firmware/` and `tools/` equal a release branch head that already passed `emu:smoke`, `emu:scenarios` and the rig's batch run (then neither the emulator nor the rig runs again on it, [D-105](ledger/releases.md#D-105), [D-120](ledger/releases.md#D-120), [D-141](ledger/releases.md#D-141)); install it on the FM-1 (`tools/fm1_install.py` or the Flash tab) for the owner's check; `npm run firmware -- build/release-1.8.0/matryoshka-1.8.0.fwsc` bundles those bytes for the Flash tab; tag `vX.Y.Z` on a commit whose `firmware/` and `tools/` equal the built commit's, and attach the `.fwsc`, `SHA256SUMS` and a zip of the folder to the GitHub release. After the tag, `npx vite-node scripts/legacy-factory.ts` then `npm run export-defs` on main add the release's factory set to `RELEASED_SETS` (`bank_add`, below), so the next firmware knows which factory patches a bank of this release already had; `legacy-factory.test.ts` fails until they do (1.11.0's was added after the fact, in 1.11.1). `build.py` writes a `<package>.json` sidecar (version, identity, size, sha256) next to every package, because the package's image is encrypted and `sync-firmware.mjs` cannot read the version from it. Since 1.0, Pages no longer builds firmware: it publishes the committed package in `web/public/firmware/`. Since 1.2 it builds the newest release tag, not main, so the site always pairs an editor with the firmware it was released with (main may bump the patch format freely); pushing the tag deploys the release, and a web-only fix is a patch release that keeps the firmware package. The changelog page alone takes `CHANGELOG.md` from main, and a push to main that changes it deploys the newest release again ([D-112](ledger/releases.md#D-112)). A new factory patch (with its XL and engine) ships the same way, as a web-only patch release, and reaches the FM-1's factory slots with the next firmware release ([D-126](ledger/releases.md#D-126)).

**Hosting (1.0).** The web app is on Cloudflare Pages, https://matryoshka.lol/ (the Pages project `matry0shka`, the custom domain set in the Cloudflare dashboard, where Cloudflare also runs the domain's DNS; the domain is registered with Amazon Route 53). The project's own address, https://matry0shka.pages.dev/ (the site's address from 1.0 to 1.4), serves the same app. There, `web/src/site-move.ts` runs first: when the origin's localStorage holds no `matryoshka.*` / `modular-1.*` key it calls `location.replace` to the same path, query and fragment at matryoshka.lol and throws, so no other module runs and nothing is stored. With data, the app stays and `web/src/move-offer.ts` shows a bar at the top, "Move them there", which runs the hand-off below with the app itself as the sender (`sendMigration`); "Not now" or a finished move hides it at that address (`matryoshka.move-offer`, never handed over), and the command palette there has "Move to matryoshka.lol". The command palette's "Save browser backup" (any address) saves the forwarder's backup file. The `deploy` workflow (`.github/workflows/pages.yml`, in glvno/matryoshka: on a pushed release tag `vX.Y.Z` when it is the newest release, by hand with an optional tag, or on a push to main that changes `CHANGELOG.md`) checks out the newest release tag and builds its `web/dist` and runs `wrangler pages deploy web/dist --project-name=matry0shka --branch=main` through `cloudflare/wrangler-action@v3` (secret `CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN`, variable `CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID`); on a manual run from main (the github-pages environment deploys only main) a second job then publishes `deploy/github-pages-forward/` to GitHub Pages, so the old address https://glvno.github.io/matryoshka/ serves only the forwarder (`index.html` and an identical `404.html`, so every old path lands on it). The address is written once in the app, `SITE_URL` in `web/src/site.ts` (share links made outside a page, the community search, index.html's link previews through `vite.config.ts`; a link made in the editor uses the address it is open at); `site.test.ts` checks that the forwarder names the same address. Elsewhere it is in the workflow (the project name), the README, the CHANGELOG, CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md and the community issue form.
- **The forwarder** strips `/matryoshka/` and keeps the rest of the path, the query and the fragment (a share link's `#p=`). With no `matryoshka.*` / `modular-1.*` key in its origin's localStorage it calls `location.replace` at once; with some it shows "Matryoshka has moved" with **Bring my patches**, **Open without them** and **Download a backup** (a JSON file `{format: 'matryoshka-browser-backup', version: 1, from, saved, keys}`).
- **The hand-off** (`web/src/migrate.ts`) is window.open + postMessage, because an iframe of the old origin inside the new app would read partitioned storage in Chrome. The button opens the new address with `?migrate=1` (a user gesture, so not a blocked popup; not `noopener`). The app, seeing `migrate=1` and a `window.opener`, posts `{type: 'matryoshka-migrate-ready'}` to the opener once for each earlier origin (`MIGRATE_FROM`: `https://glvno.github.io`, `https://matry0shka.pages.dev`; the browser delivers only the one matching the opener); the sender takes it only from the new origin and the window it opened, and replies `{type: 'matryoshka-migrate-data', keys}` with targetOrigin the new origin; the app takes that only from an origin of `MIGRATE_FROM` and its opener, imports, replies `matryoshka-migrate-done` to that origin (the forwarder then says "Done, you can close this tab") and strips `migrate=1` with `replaceState`. No reply in 5 s: it stops listening and strips `migrate=1`, quietly. The data never goes into a URL. A test build may set `VITE_MIGRATE_FROM` (the old origin) and the forwarder takes `?to=` (the new address) only when it is itself served from localhost; production keeps both origins fixed. Neither side would survive a `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy: same-origin` header on the new site (the opener would be null): do not add one.
- **The import** (`planImport`) accepts only `matryoshka.*` / `modular-1.*` keys with string values (a legacy name becomes `matryoshka.*` unless that is there too) and never overwrites silently: the lists (Your patches, the library, the bank, the browser-only bank list, tutorial variations) merge entry by entry, a different entry under a name already here is added as "name (old site)" in Your patches and the library and left out of the bank (its names are the FM-1's slots; 64 at most); any other value is taken when this page found no such key at its start, else the one here is kept (an open patch kept here puts the old one into Your patches). The page then reloads so every part reads the data (a share link's fragment is put back first), and a toast lists what came over and what was kept. The backup file goes through the same import from Load .json, or the palette's Import browser backup.
- **Analytics** are Cloudflare Web Analytics, cookieless, turned on in the Pages project's dashboard: Cloudflare adds its beacon at the edge, there is no script for it in the repository.

**Emulator.** The release firmware runs on fm1-emu (an emulated AC791N with LCD, keys, audio, flash and TRS MIDI, no USB, no P33): `npm run emu` opens it in a window, `npm run emu:smoke` drives it through boot, pages, patches, MIDI, SAVE / restore and the preset bank. Run locally before any device work; details and limits in [EMULATOR.md](EMULATOR.md).

## Open questions

- ~~Per-voice polyphony~~ done, see below; the CPU cost per voice on the device is not measured yet.
- ~~Fixed-point vs. float on pi32v2~~ settled by measurement, see below.
- See [D-263](ledger/patch-format.md#D-263) and [D-257](ledger/patch-format.md#D-257).

## Measured CPU budget (benchmark firmware, `firmware/src/bench.c`)

Per 128-frame block (2902 us of audio as counted then; 2901 us at the real 44118 Hz), interrupts off, 16 repetitions, 1 us resolution, run on a real FM-1:

| kernel | us/block | instances in 70 % of a block |
|---|---|---|
| osc, Q15 parabolic sine | 7 | 290 |
| 4x Q15 multiply-add | 9 | 225 |
| state variable filter, Q15 | 8 | 253 |
| osc, float | 489 | 4 |
| 4x float multiply-add | 444 | 4 |
| state variable filter, float | 340 | 5 |
| float to int16 per frame | 116 | 17 |

Conclusions: the core has no FPU (float links against `libcompiler-rt.a` soft float) and float is 40 to 70 times slower, so the audio path is **fixed point only** (Q15 samples, 32-bit accumulators, no float, no 64-bit division). Float is acceptable only at control rate (per block), and better avoided. The fixed kernels are tiny; real modules will cost several times their bare kernel (buffer traffic, modulation, CV), so plan for tens of module instances, and measure the real engine before promising more. The 1 us resolution makes the fixed numbers coarse (+-1 us).

## Estimated CPU load (the editor's budget meter)

The editor estimates how much of the 2901 us audio interrupt a patch needs (`web/src/cpu.ts`, `cpuEstimate`; the status line's CPU bar; over 100 % counts as over the FM-1 budget like a memory limit, `deviceBudget` in patchbin.ts). It is a model: cycles counted on a stand-in core, calibrated on whole factory patches measured on the FM-1; nothing was measured per module on the device.

- **Counting.** `tools/cpu/build.sh` builds `firmware/src/engine.c` for rv32im (clang + ld.lld, `-Os` as tools/build.py) with `tools/cpu/prof.c` as the driver; `tools/cpu/rv32.mjs` is a small RV32IM interpreter (cycles = 1 per instruction, +1 load, +2 taken branch / jump, +32 division) that reads the `ENG_PROF` hooks in `render_voice` (compiled out in the firmware) and attributes each module's input sums, parameter inputs (`apply_cv`) and processing. The FM-1's core is not RISC-V; the model only has to get the ratios right.
- **Scale and engine factors (calibrated 2026-11-08 on the test rig).** `us = cycles / 206.5 + 244` per block, of 2901 us, where each module of a factory engine counts its cycles times that engine's factor (`cyclesPerUs`, `fixedUs` and `rig.engines` in cpu-costs.json, frozen constants in cpu-costs.ts `SURVEY` and `RIG`, not re-fitted on each run). `web/scripts/cpu-rig-fit.ts` works them out from the rig's survey of every factory patch on release 1.11 ([the measurements](measurements/2026-11-08-rig-release-1.11.md): the longest audio interrupt at rest and with 1 / 4 / 8 keys held, the transport stopped and running). A patch's figure is its steady peak: the median of a phase's samples after the first, the most over its phases. A phase where the overload guard dropped parts counts at the voices that were left, and a muted phase not at all. The 808 Kit and Verona Drum 1, muted on that build and back to their 1.10 layout since ([D-879](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-879), [D-880](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-880)), take their figure from the survey of 2026-11-05 times 1.132, the median rise of the patches that did not change between the two surveys. The scale is a least-squares fit on the patches without an engine of their own (Init, Simple FM, Plucked String, the 909 Kit, `<7PH`): rig us = 1.130 x the estimate at the scale before (233.4 cycles per us + 23 us, fitted to the FM-1's survey of 2026-10-20) + 218 us. Each factory engine's factor (0.72 for Algo Four to 1.19 for Silver Box) is then the smallest that makes the estimate read at least the rig's figure for every voice count its patch played. The counted engine misses what the FM-1 spends beyond its instructions (its caches and memory, the hardware's own timing), and that differs from engine to engine. A module outside a factory engine counts at the scale alone. The old scale is still counted as a cross-check (cpu-costs.json `calibration`): the M1-0.2 engine (b907693) running its 303 Acid with a note held peaks at 84,600 cycles per block, which took 12 % (peak) of the interrupt on the FM-1 = 243 cycles per us; the bench kernels above (4x MAC 9 us, SVF 8 us) give 258 and 243. `cpu.test.ts` holds every factory patch the rig measured within -3 / +5 points of its figure; after large engine or firmware changes a new survey replaces the files and the constants.
- **Two cores (#271, calibrated 2026-11-05, [D-380](ledger/engine.md#D-380)).** The FM-1 renders the odd parts of every poly group of two or more parts on its second core, in each block where an odd and an even part both play (else core 0 renders them all, [D-391](ledger/engine.md#D-391)), so the estimate is core 0's: `us = cycles / 206.5 + 244 + 27 - 0.85 × split / 206.5`, where `split` is the modelled cycles of those odd parts (`cpuEstimate().split`; `oneCoreUs` keeps the one-core figure). 27 us is what the second core costs core 0 (24 us at the scale before), the median over the factory patches that do not split, against release-1.10 before #271 (from −31 to +80 us). 0.85 is under the least-squares 1.00, so the estimate never promises more room than the split gave on the rig (`cores` in cpu-costs.json, cpu-costs.ts `CORES`; the surveys in [the second core's measurements](measurements/2026-11-05-rig-second-core.md)). A second core given up by its fallback leaves core 0 at the one-core figure, which the overload guard handles by shedding parts.
- **Table.** `cd web && npm run cpu-costs` (`web/scripts/cpu-costs.ts`, about a minute on a 14-core Mac on a worker pool, `tools/cpu/pool.mjs`) writes `web/src/cpu-costs.json`: per module type the cost with defaults plus a grid over what changes it, found by trying every setting alone and with every input patched (choices, small integers, a number stepping above its minimum = a mode such as the oscillator's FM, numbers as interpolated points such as `coarse`, patched inputs, patched outputs); shared costs (summing, a parameter input and each parameter it moves, morph, bypass, per module, per block, per Output). Pitch inputs are fed C5 while counting (a square or saw oscillator costs up to twice as much higher up); inputs are fed by an oscillator and by a held gate, the worse counts. Rerun it after changing the engine. The counts follow the compiler: the table is made with the clang in `tools/cpu/clang-version`, and `tools/cpu/build.sh` warns when another version counts. CI does not rebuild it: commit the table after an engine change ([D-383](ledger/engine.md#D-383)).
- **Checks.** Every factory patch and factory assembly (in a new patch, its outputs into the Output and Main's keys / pitch / gate / velocity into its inputs, so it is heard and played) is counted whole, holding one note per voice of its largest poly group (idle voices are skipped, below; the estimate is the worst case, every voice playing), and its peak includes the note-on block; `cpu.test.ts` holds the model to those counts (never more than 5 % low, at most 15 % high). Exempt from the upper bound (`looseModel` in cpu-checks.ts): grain and compressor (their cost depends on what they are fed; the table holds the worst case, a slowly triggered grain module reads up to 3x high), a filter cut-off moved by an LFO, an LFO whose rate is patched (a knob may turn it up to where the pitch it modulates moves every sample), an envelope or glide into a pitch (charged as moving all block; it settles sooner).
- **Factory patches against the test rig** (`cd web && npx vite-node scripts/cpu-rig-fit.ts` prints this table and the constants): the rig's steady peak against the estimate at the scale before (233.4 cycles per us + 23 us, no factors) and after. The estimate is the worst case, every voice playing; a row with fewer voices than the patch has comes from a phase where the overload guard dropped parts, and one with more from a phase with fewer keys than voices (an idle voice costs little). Every factory patch the rig measured reads within -3 / +5 points of it (`cpu.test.ts`). `CPU_WARN` stays at 80 %. A VOICES row stops at the most voices whose estimate is at most 90 % (`CPU_VOICE_TOP`, [D-870](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-870)).

  | patch | voices | rig | before | after |
  |---|---:|---:|---:|---:|

- **USB audio is not modelled.** The survey was taken without the USB audio stream (it came later); streaming adds about 2 points (Algo Four 86 -> 88 %). The estimate leaves it out.
- **Per module (model figures at default settings, calibrated on whole patches; not measured per module on the device).** `run + cv` of cpu-costs.json plus the per-module render cost (`node`, 209 cycles), at the calibrated scale (without a factory engine's factor); one instance, nothing patched, pitch at C5. The fixed costs come on top once per patch: the block (4312 cycles) and the 244 us, about 9.1 % together, plus 3745 cycles (0.6 %) per Output. Settings, patched inputs and outputs change most of these (the grid in cpu-costs.json; the oscillator's FM mode or unison, a filter's moving cut-off), and each part of a poly assembly counts again.

- **Later, with a second FM-1:** a build that times the `ENG_PROF` stages with `fm1_ticks` and reports them over SysEx can replace the counts with measured microseconds (`CpuTable.source`, `cyclesPerUs` = 1).

- **Oscillator increments** (#461, [D-356](ledger/engine.md#D-356)): the oscillators (Oscillator, Wavetable, phase distortion) work out their phase increment (`exp2_scale`) exactly at every 8th sample of a block (`INC_SPAN`) and follow a straight line in between, in both engines (`inc_span` / `INC_LOOP` in engine.c, `incRamp` in modules.ts). The line in a span runs toward the increment at the next span's first sample (the block's last sample for the last span). Only these anchor samples count: a pitch that is the same at both ends of a span gives that span the exact increment, so a pitch that holds still sounds exactly as before. A vibrato costs one exp2 per 8 samples instead of one per sample; against working it out every sample, the largest change a 20 Hz, half-octave vibrato makes in any bin of the spectrum is 66 dB below the strongest partial (golden kits `vibrato-osc`, `vibrato-wt-pd`; `vibrato-steps` jumps the pitch mid-block). A pitch from Main or a Part holds still for a block, so it costs less than one that moves. The table's `moving` (cycles per block and unison copy, measured with an LFO added to the pitch) is charged by `cpuEstimate` when the pitch may change about every sample (`keepsMoving` = `slope` ≥ 0.5 Q16 steps per sample: oscillators, noise, envelopes, glides, Random walks anywhere upstream; an LFO by its rate x depth unless something is patched into it; a free sample-and-hold LFO steps once a cycle and is not charged; a Scale multiplies its input's slope by its gain).

### Where the cycles go, and the speed-ups (2026-10-18, branch `cpu-opt`)

Profiled with `cd web && npm run cpu-hot` (the counted peak of every check, `--save` / `--compare` for before / after, `CPU_ELF=` another build, `CPU_NOTES=n` fewer keys), `-- --types` (cycles per module type over every check), `-- --nodes NAME` (per module of one check), `-- --hot NAME` (per function and per source line, from a `-g` build symbolized by `llvm-symbolizer`; inlined code counts where it was written). `scripts/cpu-est-check.ts NAME` shows the model's view of a check, `scripts/cpu-model-vs.ts F.json` the model's error against saved counts.

- **Before (main at 9cb3053), over every check:** module work 83 %, parameter inputs 9 %, input sums 1 %, the block loop and the Output 7 %. By type: SVF filter 21 % (360 cycles per sample with its cv patched: per sample `svf_g` + `exp2_scale` + two `svf_stage`, each a 32-bit division), oscillators 14 %, the reverb 10 % (142 k cycles, a fifth of the budget, each), ADSR 7 %, the Main module 7 % (it filled twelve output buffers every block, plugged or not), modal 7 %, LFO 4 %. The software 64-bit division (`__udivmoddi4`, the compiler's call for every `int64 /`) was 6..12 % of a patch: `apply_cv`'s block means, every per-block setup (cut-off and rates in Hz, the reverb's decay, the modal body's modes, the part flaws), the poly-BLEP. The `mulq` / `mulr` helpers were not inlined at `-Os`.
- **Bit-exact speed-ups** (`tools/engine_exact.sh` renders every golden kit and every factory patch (`firmware/test/exact_factory.c`, eight keys held, then released) with the old and the new engine and compares them sample for sample; `firmware/test/exact_test.c` checks the division-free helpers against plain division on random and edge inputs): `HOT` (always inline) multiply helpers; `block_mean` (sum / 128 by shift, rounding as C does); `hz_turns` (Hz -> turns per sample in two 32-bit divisions); `div64.c` (64-bit division in 32-bit steps, Hacker's Delight `divlu` / `divdu`; it replaces libcompiler-rt's members in the link, so every 64-bit division in the firmware is faster, checked against the host's division); summed inputs copy the first source instead of zeroing; the ladder's states in registers and its stages written out; the oscillator and the free-running LFO pick their wave once a block (no `switch` per sample), skip sync handling when nothing is patched there, and get the poly-BLEP position from a reciprocal cached per increment plus one exact correction (`blep_pos`); the SVF works out its coefficients only when the cv value changes and its second stage only when `lp24` or `mix` is used; the reverb's and the modal body's states in locals (int16 / int32 stores could alias them) with the lines / modes written out; Main and Part skip fills into unconnected outputs. The 1.11.1 pass (#781): the Envelope, Env 8 and ADSR write one level at rest, hold and sustain until the gate changes, and run a moving segment in a tight loop up to the sample that ends it, with Env 8's cached rates in locals (its output could alias them); an unweighted input sum adds three or four sources a pass; the SVF solves stage 1 once a sample with drive or self-oscillation, not twice when its cv moves; the Mixer multiplies only the inputs that add something (an unplugged one, a level of 0: six mixers at level 0 carry Briefcase 3's preset card); Math with up to three outputs in use runs a pass for each.
- **After the 1.11.1 pass, on the test rig** ([the measurements](measurements/2026-11-08-rig-speed-pass.md)): the steady peak against 1.11.0, Briefcase 3 90.8 % -> 75.9 %, Algo Four 86.6 % -> 77.3 %, Cassia 1 (two voices) 61.9 % -> 53.3 %, Verona Drum 1 83.3 % -> 77.8 %, Oracle V 78.1 % -> 73.8 %, the 808 Kit 82.4 % -> 78.6 %. On the rig about two thirds of the counted saving shows (the rest of the block's time does not change). Cassia 1 went back to four voices (77 %); Oracle V and Hera at five voices still shed one, and the 808 Kit with its toms and Verona Drum 1 with DRUM 2 and PAN are still muted after a load (#782).
- **Idle voices** (not bit-exact, the same rule in both engines, golden agrees): a part of a poly group whose gate is low, whose ADSR / level envelopes (at least one) have finished and whose outputs out of the group stayed within 6 / 65536 for a whole block goes idle: those outputs are zeroed and the part's modules are skipped until a note reaches it, except every 32nd block, which runs it and checks again (a part that sounds without its envelopes comes back within ~93 ms). Each part checks in its own one of those 32 blocks: part v of poly group g (both counted from 0) when the engine's block count plus 8 g + v is a multiple of 32. So parts that went idle together, which is every part after a patch loads, do not all run in the same block (#434: before, drift poly at 8 voices overran every 32nd block with one note held). A voice that sat idle starts its next note with its oscillators and LFOs where they stopped (free-running phases differ from before; 20 ms loudness within ~0.3..1 dB on the poly golden kits, up to ~4 dB in the beating of detuned voices). The estimate stays the worst case (every voice playing); with one key down Oracle V counts 19 % instead of 65 %.
- **Overload guard** (`firmware/src/audio.c`, the decision in `ovl_guard.h`, host test `tools/guard_test.sh`; [D-362](ledger/engine.md#D-362), issue #458): the audio interrupt times itself (the render plus the key scan and USB nested in it) and checks after each half buffer whether the hardware already plays the half it just wrote (late). It drops a part (`eng_shed`, [shedding](ledger/vocabulary.md#shedding)) when the mean time (each half moves it by an eighth) passes 90 % of a half, or when a half comes out late a second time within 2 s (688 halves); the first two halves after a patch load do not count as late. After a drop the mean restarts at 7/8 of its limit. DIAG page 119 reports what it sees (halves over 85 %, late halves, the longest gap between interrupts, its mean; `tools/rig_cpu_survey.py` records the mean and the late halves).
- **Device compile pass** (`web/src/device-compile.ts`, the last step of `deviceView(p, true)`, used by `encodePatch`, `deviceBudget`, `cpuEstimate`, `deviceIndex`; the editor's engine runs the uncompiled patch so unplugged modules still show live values): modules nothing audible depends on are not sent (except before the last Random module, whose seed is its index); modules of a poly group whose inputs come only from outside the group (or from such modules) and whose outputs stay inside it, which read nothing per voice (not Part / Random / Main / Output, not a filter / ladder that may track the pitch, not a flaw target when the group has flaws) run once instead of once per voice. `device-compile.test.ts` renders every factory patch and assembly with and without it, sample for sample equal.
- **After, every check:** −28.6 % on average (Oracle V 91 % -> 65 %, Hera 6 84 % -> 55 %, Expander S 88 % -> 57 %, supersaw stack 88 % -> 47 %, Algo Four 92 % -> 81 %: its FM oscillators run the 2x oversampled path, not yet reworked). With that headroom the factory Oracle V went to 5 voices (81 %) and Hera 6 to 6 (77 %). Real-firmware check: the CI build (JieLi toolchain, `div64.c` in the link) of this branch and of main played the same 24 s script in fm1-emu with byte-identical audio before idle voices; the firmware's own CPU readout showed Oracle V's chord at 37 % instead of 47 %.
- **fm1-emu gap found on the way:** the JieLi compiler emitted an `if ((R & #h) == #i) goto` with a long forward branch that fm1-emu (7ca5b96) decodes only up to 13 offset bits (`if_r_h_eq_i_goto_i`, mask 0xe000.. instead of 0x8000..); the free-running LFO was moved into its own function so the branch is short. Worth reporting upstream.

## Panel buttons, saved state, LCD (M1-0.9)

- **OCT- / OCT+** (matrix buttons 0 / 1) shift the keys by up to three octaves (the shifted button's LED breathes, faster the further from 0: ±1 slow, ±2 medium, ±3 fast; a message `OCTAVE +1` shows in the top bar); a held key ends the note it started. They act when let go ([D-245](ledger/device-ui.md#D-245)): a release does nothing if the other OCT was pressed while it was held (`oct_used`), and both down opens the controls sheet (below, "Controls sheet and quick start"). **SAVE** (button 9) writes the current state to flash: the patch with its edited parameters, the octave, the selected module and the preset index (the unused words of the current-patch header). The other buttons: see [D-203](ledger/device-ui.md#D-203) and [D-219](ledger/device-ui.md#D-219). The button ids are Felucca's panel table; only PLAY and REC there were measured on hardware.
- **LCD** (`firmware/src/ui.c`, previewed on the host with `tools/ui_preview.sh OUT.png PRESET [MODULE [OCTAVE [PAGE [ARP_STEP [MENU_TAB [GUIDE_LINE [POLY]]]]]]]`): a top bar with the preset name (black on the accent colour; messages replace it for a moment), a picture of the patch as a vertical strip on the right third (modules as coloured boxes in rows by depth, cables as lines, the selected module framed), the selected module laid out like the arpeggiator pages (small label, value at 1.5x size, value bar per knob row), and the two parameter inputs with their attenuverters and assignments. Choice parameters show their names (SAW, TRIANGLE, S&H), numbers their units (180 HZ, 3.19 OCT) and the value the sound really uses (base plus modulation). With the FM-1 panel selected the rows name where each knob goes (`K1 FILTER CUTOFF`, found from the cables and the target's matrix) and show the knob's position as a bar. **One line buffer for all drawing** (`lcd_line` in `firmware/src/lcd.c`, 240 pixels): the LCD's DMA reads only RAM and goes on reading a transfer's source after `lcd_fill` / `lcd_blit` return (`lcd_busy`), until `lcd_sync()` (or the next LCD access) waits for it. Every renderer that builds pixels in RAM (`lcd_fill`, the text of font5x7.c / font_bc.c, `pages_bar`, the toast, the splash, the battery mark) builds them in `lcd_line` and calls `lcd_sync()` before each write into it; `lcd_line_out(x, y, w)` swaps a built scanline to big endian in place and sends it. No renderer keeps a row buffer of its own (up to 480 B of RAM each; there were nine, 4.2 KB of the 24 KB app RAM). Nothing may draw from `lcd_yield` (the keys taken inside `lcd_sync` during a redraw, #230), so a row being built is never overwritten there. The host stand-ins (`firmware/test/host_ui.h`) keep a copy of what each transfer from `lcd_line` sent and count a change made before the next sync; `tools/panel_test.sh` fails on any.

## Reverb, logarithmic cutoff, default knob routing

- **Reverb module**: was Freeverb-style (four damped combs into two allpasses, 12 KB); now the FDN described under "Reverb (FDN)" below, same ports plus `pre` and `mod`.
- **Logarithmic parameters**: `ParamDef.log` (the filter cutoff): parameter inputs, the editor slider and the knobs move it in octaves, so a fraction of the range is a fraction of the octaves (`modulate` in `patch.ts`, `eng_step_param` and the log2 / exp2 tables in `engine.c`).
- **Factory patches** (`web/presets/NN-name.json`, written by `web/scripts/gen-presets.ts` from the factory assemblies; run `gen-assemblies.ts` first, then `npm run export-defs`). Since 2026-10-18 they are all our own: Oracle V, three simple ones (Init, Simple FM, Plucked String), instruments built after famous architectures (Monolith D: Minimoog Model D; Silver Box: TB-303; Hera 4: Juno-106, four voices (Hera 6 XL plays six); Sallen 20: MS-20; Expander S: Oberheim SEM / Four Voice; Algo Four: 4-op DX / TX; Canvas: Buchla Music Easel; Wanderer: ARP Odyssey; Briefcase 3: EMS VCS3; Io 4: Roland Jupiter-4; Vernon 100: EML ElectroComp 100; 8 Lire: SOMA Lyra-8, eight fixed-tuned voices on a Drum map's rows, #182; Elvira 2: neutral labs Elmyra 2; Scrounge: neutral labs Scrooge, a drum engine with its pattern on a Steps track; Meridian 8: Landscape NOON, the same way; Perun 4: Erica Synths PERKONS HD-01, the same way; Spokes 6: Elektron Model:Cycles, six FM tracks, its drums on a DRUMS track and its two melodic voices on the pitch and gate of a TONE and a CHORD track, [D-346](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-346); Cassia 1: Casio CZ-1, two lines of a PD Osc each, every envelope an Env 8; it was Cassia 1 XL until it fit, and it still leaves parts of the CZ-1 out (four of its eight voices), [D-348](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-348)), the 808 Kit, the 909 Kit (kept as its file) and <7PH (built from the 909 Kit: its DRUMS track and kit, plus the Silver Box on its own PATTERN track, a 16-step acid line in B flat minor at 124 BPM; Main's keys into PATTERN only, so they play the bass and reach the kit when DRUMS is armed; the bass on the kit mixer's in4), then Spokes 6 and Cassia 1, then Basic Poly, Drift Poly, Mono Lead and Supersaw Stack (slots 25..28: the simple engines that have a preset card, each its factory assembly as made with Main's keys into it, so that their presets can be reached on the FM-1, [D-853](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-853); new factory patches go last, [D-334](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-334)), then Verona Drum 1 (slot 29: the Vermona DRM1 MKIV drum machine, seven of its eight channels with every knob of each and a PAN each, on a PATTERN track; Verona Drum 1 XL has all eight, [D-862](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-862), [D-863](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-863)). Each instrument is a factory assembly built like the original's panel (columns, headings, envelope displays, a scope), the patch plugs Main's keys (or pitch / gate) and mod wheel into it. **Knobs:** see [D-313](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-313). The Output's level and each engine preset's **level row** come from a loudness render: one key at about −18 LUFS, a 4-key chord at most −15, the drums on their pattern ([D-860](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-860), [D-861](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-861)). Nothing clips: not the patch, nor any factory preset of its engines, with as many keys held as it has voices (at most 8), its Drum map's keys or its patterns running ([D-854](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-854)). Every one of these must fit the FM-1 (the generator refuses one that does not; an **XL** is the browser's full version, below); knobs on per-voice modules cost CPU per voice (a pin is ~3.4 k cycles per copy), and a knob that could switch a module into a costlier mode (an oscillator's feedback) is avoided. **Voices** (2026-11-08, the test rig's survey of release 1.11 and the CPU model refitted on it, [D-870](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-870)): Oracle V plays 4 (79 % estimated, the rig 79 %; at 5 the overload guard dropped one), Hera 4 4 (the Juno-106's whole panel: 89 % estimated, the rig 86 %; at 5, as Hera 5, the guard dropped one or two), Cassia 1 4 (78 %, the rig 77 %, since the speed pass of #781; in 1.11.0 two, when at 3 or 4 the guard dropped voices, and 3 costs core 0 what 4 does: two PD Osc and five Env 8 per voice), Expander S, Io 4 and Plucked String 4, Algo Four 8 (the DX100's count with its eight algorithms: 87 %, the rig 86 %, [D-740](ledger/fidelity.md#D-740)), Wanderer and Vernon 100 2.
- **XLs** ([D-338](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-338)): the full browser version of a factory patch that was cut down to fit the FM-1, named after it with " XL" ("Algo Four XL"). Each is one file, `web/scripts/xl/<slug>.ts`, exporting `xl` (`Xl` in `web/scripts/xl/xl.ts`: `base`, the FM-1 version's name, and `build(fm1)`, which gets a fresh copy of the FM-1 version to start from), and one line in `web/scripts/xl/index.ts`. `npx vite-node scripts/gen-xl.ts` (after `gen-presets.ts`) writes them to `web/presets/xl/<slug>.json` and logs each one's budget: the FM-1's budget does not bind an XL, so one over it is a sandbox patch, and one that fits is allowed. The shared helpers of both generators (`around`, `level`, `players`, ...) are in `web/scripts/factory-build.ts`. An XL that needs an assembly of its own (8 Lire XL, Elvira 2 XL, Meridian 8 XL, Algo Four XL, Perun 4 XL, Spokes 6 XL, Expander S XL, Verona Drum 1 XL) builds it with `web/scripts/xl/def.ts` (Verona Drum 1 XL from the same channel builders as its FM-1 version, `web/scripts/verona-voices.ts`): a definition that lives only in the XL, never on the assembly shelves, with Macro pins that turn several parameters at once (one switch of the original), nested racks of voice cards (`nest`'s `poly`), and a level matched to the FM-1 version's loudness (`matchLevel`). The 909 Kit XL changes a copy of the FM-1 version's patch: new voice assemblies from `def.ts` in place of its perc card. An XL that extends its FM-1 version's assembly (Oracle V XL, Hera 6 XL, Briefcase 3 XL) edits a copy of it with `web/scripts/xl/asm-edit.ts` (`edit`: modules added, cables moved, a module changed to another type with the same ports, card rows rebound or added after the FM-1 version's pages; `aroundDef` puts it in a patch). `web/src/presets.ts` keeps them apart: `FM1_PRESETS` is the FM-1's factory patches (`web/presets/*.json`, slot order), `XL_PRESETS` the XLs, and `PRESETS` the browser's list, each XL right after its FM-1 version (`withXls`). Everything that reaches the FM-1 reads `FM1_PRESETS` only: `export-defs` (`presets.h`, so also the legacy factory fingerprints and the emulated PRESET), the bank (`bank.ts`), the golden kits, the CPU survey checks and the emulator level script. Every XL matches its original but for the gaps its header lists ([D-347](ledger/patches-and-presets.md#D-347)); one over the share link limit is shared as a stored link.

## Polyphony: Voice allocators and poly assemblies (format 20; allocators 2026-10-17)

Polyphony works the way a polysynth's hardware does (2026-10-17, replacing the Part module and the voice settings on the assembly). The **Main** module's `keys` output is the keyboard's ribbon cable: the held keys, a jack kind of its own (`SignalKind` `keys`, only into a keys input: `kindsFit` in patch.ts). A **Voice allocator** module (`alloc`, modules.ts) is the CPU: its `keys` input takes Main's keys, its parameters `voices` (1..16; the FM-1 plays 8), `assign` rotate / lowest / reuse / reset, `legato` auto / off / on, `priority` last / low / high, `unison` and `steal` decide how the keys become voices, and its outputs `pitch` (with the bend), `gate`, `vel`, `index` (−1 first voice .. 1 last, 0 with one), `legato` (1 while the note began over a held one) and `jump` (the pitch step from the voice's previous note, in octaves) are per voice. An allocator without Main's keys plays nothing. Its `assign` and `unison` inputs (#681, [D-771](ledger/modules.md#D-771)) set those two settings by a cable: a signal moves a setting by whole choices, one a unit, rounded to the nearest (`voicingSetting`, modules.ts). Both engines read each input's first sample once a block, after the block (`engine.ts` `voicingCables`, `engine.c` `group_cables`), so the next block's keys follow it. When unison turns on, the notes the voices play, oldest first, become the held keys; when it turns off, each voice keeps its note until its key is let go (`unisonSwitch`, `unison_switch`). `deviceView` hands such a cable to each of the allocator's Parts and gives the allocator a group of its own, and a patch with one is written in format 51 (`uses111`, patchbin.ts). One Selector row is then a polysynth's whole key assign switch: Io 4's KEY ASSIGNMENT, Hera 6's assign mode, Algo Four's POLY / MONO (unison with `stack` 1). A **poly assembly** (`AssemblyInst.poly` = `PolyCards`: only the cards' `flaws`) is a rack of voice cards: its member modules, nested plain assemblies included, run once per voice of the allocator that feeds it (`allocatorOf`: the first allocator with a cable into a member that runs per voice, else one inside it), voice i on card i; one that no allocator feeds runs once. Delay, reverb, chorus, granular and the Output (`SHARED_TYPES`) and allocators run once even inside one. A poly assembly cannot sit inside another. Rules and helpers: `web/src/poly.ts` (`polyGroups`, `copiesOf`, `allocatorOf`, `deviceView`, `polyOfModule`, `setPoly`, `makeRoom`).

**Signals.** An allocator's output into a poly assembly it drives reaches voice i on card i; into a module that runs once it is the sum of its voices. A cable leaving a poly assembly carries the **sum of its cards** (a shared reverb hears the chord); a cable entering one reaches **every card**, as a polysynth's panel reaches every voice (one LFO sweeps all notes). Between two poly assemblies the receiving cards each get the sum of the sending ones. **Paraphonic mix (#125):** when no card gates its own sound (nothing inside the assembly hears the allocator's gate or legato: the gate goes only to modules that run once, say one shared envelope, as in Vernon 100), every card sounds whether it is played or not; its audio leaving the assembly is then weighed by 1 / cards (Q16, engine.ts `paraMix` = engine.c `para_w`), a fixed mixer, so the voice count does not change the level. Cards that gate themselves (an envelope or a gate-driven VCA inside) are summed as they are. One allocator may drive several poly assemblies (the same voices, layered: a Prophet-10's two layers); several allocators may share Main's keys (a pad and a bass, each with its own voice count).

**Device form** (`deviceView`). The engines and the FM-1 run the form from before allocators: every allocator becomes a **Part** module (`part`, engine only, not offered in the editor) in each poly group it drives; the first takes the allocator's id and place in the module list (module indices of the other modules stay the patch's: Live parameter changes address them), the others are appended. Each group carries the allocator's settings plus its cards' flaws (`PolyCfg`) and allocates the notes itself; groups with the same settings allocate the same way. An allocator's cables into modules outside its groups come from its first group's Part (the sum); one that drives no group gets a group of its own, or, with one voice played the keyboard's way, is a Part outside any group (the shared keyboard). Keys cables and allocators without keys are dropped. The binary (format 21), the C engine, the budget meter and the CPU estimate all take this form, so `firmware/` did not change; the golden kits' binaries and renders were byte-identical before and after. **Unison**: every voice plays the one note the priority picks (`monoUpdate` / `mono_update` on every part, with the same stamps); bit 7 of the group record's legato byte, and only then is the binary format 22 (`ENG_PATCH_VERSION` 22, compat 21; a patch without unison stays format 21 byte for byte). Golden kit `alloc-unison`.

**Notes.** Each poly group gets every note. Everything outside the poly groups runs once and plays the **shared keyboard**: Main's `pitch` / `gate` / `vel`, one note, the last held, legato. The editor's **poly** button (`setPoly`) puts a Voice allocator inside the assembly (it runs once there and travels with the assembly into the library), patches Main's keys into it (a `keys` jack on the card) and moves the Main pitch / gate / vel cables into the assembly onto it; turning poly off moves them back. The assembly card's settings show the voice count (the allocator's, read only there) and the flaws; the allocator's card has the rest. Older patches and definitions are converted on load (`migrateParts`: each Part becomes an allocator in its place with its poly assembly's old settings, or one voice outside one, and Main's keys go into it; `migrateDefParts` exposes its keys on the definition's card).

**Allocation** (`engine.ts groupOn` / `monoUpdate`, `engine.c group_on` / `mono_update`, identical). One part: the held keys form a list, the sounding one is the last, lowest or highest held (`priority`); a change of key while the gate is up is legato (`legato` auto and on keep the envelopes running, off restarts them). Several parts: a key already down on a part keeps it; else a free part by `assign` (rotate: the next in turn; lowest: the lowest-numbered; reuse: a free part whose last note was the key, else the one free the longest); with none free one is **taken over** by `priority` (last: the oldest note; low / high: the highest / lowest sounding note, and only for a lower / higher key, otherwise the key does not sound). A taken-over part outputs one low gate sample at the start of the next block (`retrig`, cleared after every block) unless `legato` is on, so its envelopes restart.

**Reset and no-steal allocation.** `assign: reset` rotates while keys overlap and starts again at voice one after every input key is released. A key whose voice was stolen or refused still counts as held. With `steal: off`, a new key is ignored when every polyphonic voice is held. It is not queued for later. A repeated key keeps its voice, and a released voice is available immediately. Single-voice and unison modes retain their note-priority behavior. Defaults keep stealing on and preserve the three existing assignment modes. See [D-441](ledger/modules.md#D-441), Proposed.

**Unison count.** Voicing's `stack` parameter selects how many of its existing voices play the priority note in unison. Zero means all voices. The polyphonic count stays unchanged, and a count larger than that pool uses the whole pool. Editing the count updates held unison notes immediately; the device does not reload the patch. The appended `unison` output reports the mode independently of the gate. Oracle V XL exposes `uni voices` and gates its detune and manual chord offsets with that output. Chord learning remains separate work. See [D-443](ledger/modules.md#D-443), Proposed.

**Shared unison.** The `share` choice divides the unison pool equally over held notes, with spare voices released. Last, low or high priority chooses notes when more keys are held than available voices. The selected notes are assigned in ascending pitch order. Io 4 therefore plays four voices for one key, two each for two keys, and one each for three or four keys. This is its UNISON II behavior; the full four-way assignment interface is still separate work. Both engines retain all 128 MIDI keys in their held-note lists. See [D-444](ledger/modules.md#D-444), Proposed.

**Lowest/highest voice outputs.** Voicing and Part append `low` and `high` gates. They mark the lowest and highest assigned notes among held voices in each group. Released voices and refused notes do not qualify. A mono voice is both, and every tied unison voice qualifies. Existing outputs retain their indices and behavior. Algo Four XL uses these gates for its named LOW/HIGH/KON pitch-bend control. KON applies bend to every voice, including release tails. Its breath and foot inputs use Main CC 2 and CC 64. See [D-442](ledger/modules.md#D-442), Proposed.

**Random.** The **Random** module (`mode` fixed / walk, `rate`) gives each copy its own value −1..1: fixed is `hash32` of the instance seed (`instSeed` = module index and part), walk an integer random walk (`walkInit` / `walkStep`: a new Q15 target every `hold` = round(sr / (128 · rate)) blocks, each block 2 / hold of the way there; it steps every block in either mode). Bit for bit the same in both engines. It replaces the Main module's former `tol` / `drift` outputs and the oscillator's `drift`: a fixed Random into a cutoff is a voice card's tolerance, a walking one into `fine` an oscillator's drift.

**Part flaws** (2026-10-16, replacing the `unit` seed): each part of a poly assembly has its own flaws, set by hand in the editor's flaws card (`web/src/flaws-panel.ts`, opened from `flaws` in the poly settings; Randomize gives every part small ones). `PolyCfg.flaws[i]` = `PartFlaw` of part i (patch.ts; missing = none): `detune` −50..50 cents and `drift` 0..100 % (a slow walk, `driftSeed(group, part)`, a new target every 2 s (`DRIFT_RATE` 0.5 Hz), up to ±20 cents at 100 %) are added to that part's Part `pitch` output (`flawQ16`, integer and the same in both engines); `bright` −24..24 semitones multiplies the cutoff of every filter / ladder in the part (2^(st/12)), `env` −50..50 % the a / d / r of every ADSR and r1..r4 of every envelope ((100 + env) / 100), `level` 0..100 % the gain of every VCA (`FLAW_TARGETS`), on the copy's effective value after modulation, clamped to the range (engine.ts `applyFlaws`, engine.c `apply_flaws`). Golden kit `part-flaws` (every flaw at its ends); dropping detune or `apply_flaws` in C fails it.

**Old patches** are converted on load (`migrateVoicing` in poly.ts, inside `validatePatch`): `voices` > 1, non-default Main legato / priority or cables from spread / legato / jump become an open poly assembly "basic poly" (assign reuse, the old allocation) around the old per-voice set plus a Part; Main tol / drift become Random modules; an oscillator's drift becomes a walking Random into `fine` through a free pin (depth = drift / 2 / 200). Saved assembly definitions get the same treatment (`migrateDefDrift`). A stored `unit` (format 20) is dropped.

**Binary format 21.** Header byte 3 is the number of poly groups G (0..4); after the 8 header bytes come G records of `GROUP_REC` = 44 bytes (parts, assign, legato, priority, then for each of 8 parts: i8 detune, u8 drift, i8 bright, i8 env, u8 level), then, with any group, one byte per module: its group (0 = runs once). The module records start at `eng_records_at` (engine.h). Stored patches older than `ENG_PATCH_COMPAT_VERSION` (31 today) are refused, and the slot plays the fallback patch ([D-251](ledger/patch-format.md#D-251)).

**Engines.** `engine.ts`: a `Group` per poly assembly (plus the keyboard), a node has one `Inst` per part of its group, `gather` sums another group's instances. `engine.c`: `grp_t E.grp[0..ngrp]` (0 = the keyboard) with their parts in `E.pv[]` (`ENG_NPV` 33), `node_t.grp`, `cur_g` / `cur_pv` while a node's instance renders; inputs from another group's per-part output get one summing source per part.

**Limits on the device.** Signal buffers: 255 (`ENG_NBUF`), one per connected output and instance plus one per summed input; instances 128 (`ENG_MAX_INST`). `eng_load` returns -6 / -7 when a patch does not fit. `deviceBudget` in `web/src/patchbin.ts` counts the same way (copies per poly assembly) and the poly settings' `parts` picker shows it (red when too big). CPU per part on the device: not measured.

**Controls.** Editor: the poly button on every assembly header, the settings as pickers on the collapsed card and as a strip under an expanded frame's header; ×N badges on the cards that run per part. Device: see [D-220](ledger/device-ui.md#D-220).

**Golden vectors.** The kits that used `voices`, Main voicing or oscillator drift are built the old way and loaded through the conversion (`makeOld` in `web/scripts/golden.ts`); `poly-layers` (three layered groups with different assign / priority / legato, a note sequence that tells the assign modes apart, cross-group FM, a shared filter and delay) and `part-random` (Part index, fixed and walking Random, part flaws, a Random outside the group) and `part-flaws` use poly assemblies directly. Changing any byte of a group record makes them fail.

## FM oscillator

The oscillator has two ways to make FM-style timbres: the `pm` inputs (phase modulation by other signals: `pm`, and since format 49 `pm2` and `pm3`, each with its own index in radians per unit of input, 0..8 for the parameter inputs, macro ranges and the morph, up to 16 on the card and the FM-1's knobs as a stretch, `ParamDef.top`; an oscillator into one swings by its `level`, so the phase moves by level × index radians; the `index` input, when patched, multiplies the sum per sample, so an envelope shapes the index without a VCA; with `pm2`, `pm3` or `index` patched both engines sum the three into one signal in radians first, modules.ts `oscPm`, engine.c `osc_pm`, and run the FM path with index 1; with only `pm` patched they run it as before) and the `fb` parameter (operator feedback: the raw wave modulates its own phase, by pi radians per unit of the average of the last two samples at `fb` = 1, the DX7's level 7). Feedback inside the module replaces the one-block-late feedback cable for this purpose.

**FM mode.** When `pm` is connected with an index above 0, or `fb` is above 0, the oscillator runs at twice the sample rate: two waves per frame (the `pm` input interpolated linearly between frames), then a polyphase half-band decimator of four first-order allpass sections, two per path (coefficients Q16 `5376 17567 35764 55599`, flat to 0.22 fs, 41 dB down above 0.28 fs: 19.4 kHz and 24.7 kHz at 44.1 kHz). Sidebands between the old Nyquist and the oversampled one are filtered out instead of folding back; on bright high notes the non-harmonic (aliased) energy drops from -27..-33 dB to about -41 dB, the decimator's floor. Both engines use the same coefficients; the fixed-point one keeps the allpass states in Q16 (`inst_t.s.osc`). Cost: about 2.3 oscillators. The plain path (no pm, no feedback) is unchanged, so patches without FM render exactly as before.

Two things to know when patching: the band-limited signal overshoots the level (up to about 1.4x on very bright settings), so leave headroom before the output; and the feedback loop runs at the oversampled rate, so the sound for a given `fb` is a little brighter than the same number on a 44.1 kHz implementation.

Binary patch format 7: the oscillator record grew by the `fb` parameter.

## Assemblies (editor only)

`web/src/assembly.ts` is the model, `web/src/assemblies.ts` the user's library (Library > Assemblies) and the factory set (`web/src/assemblies/*.json`), `editor.ts` the canvas side. The patch stays flat: `Patch.assemblies` is an overlay of entries `{ v, id, name, label, x, y, open, modules, inputs, outputs, params }` where `modules` are ids in `patch.modules`, `inputs` / `outputs` are exposed inner ports (`{ module, port, label }`) and `params` promoted inner parameters (`{ module, param, label }`). Cables always join inner ports, so the engine, `encodePatch`, the protocol and the firmware only read three things of the overlay: an assembly's `poly` setting (see Polyphony), the device pages and the device extras ([D-253](ledger/patch-format.md#D-253)); `fixAssemblies` (called by the editor when a patch comes in) drops entries that do not fit the modules and reports them. Members are plain modules, never the panel or an output, and never in two assemblies; `v` is there so an entry can later name another assembly as a member.

Rules: `reconcile` keeps an assembly consistent after every structural change (departed members leave, every inner port with an outside cable is exposed, labels of ports that stay are kept); `hidePort` refuses while an outside cable uses the port; a module that joins (`addMember`) retracts the exposures that only its cables had caused. A saved assembly (`AssemblyDef`, `format: "matryoshka-assembly"`) is a small patch with local ids 1..n, positions from the top left member, the cables between members, exposed ports and promoted parameters, parameter values as defaults, p1 / p2 matrix and attenuverters, and no K1..K4 assignments (also stripped on insert, since knobs belong to the patch). `insertAssembly` gives fresh ids, places the collapsed card at the next free grid slot; there is no module cap (the budget meter flags a patch over the FM-1's 128 modules, [D-625](ledger/editor.md#D-625)). A patch's own modules (`EXCLUDED_TYPES`: `fm1`, `output`) never belong to a building block: `makeAssembly` refuses them, the editor's "Make assembly" drops them from the selection first ("Main and Output stay outside"), and `validateAssemblyDef` runs `stripPatchIO` on every file or library entry (the modules, their cables, exposed ports and promoted parameters go). `AssemblyDef.category` is a factory definition's shelf (`CATEGORIES` in assemblies.ts: Drums, Voices, Effects, Textures, Utilities, set per `asm()` in gen-assemblies.ts; the user's own have none and sit on "Your library"). The toolbar's "Insert assembly" opens `AssemblyBrowser` (browser.ts: shelves left, entries right with `summary` and `portsOf`, a fuzzy search across shelves via fuzzy.ts, Enter or a click inserts); the module select inserts on pick and is grouped by `MODULE_GROUPS` in main.ts. "New patch" = `newPatch(DEFS)`: Main at the top left, Output three grid columns right, unwired (`emptyPatch` stays for tests); outside the tutorial it first opens "Start with an engine" (engine-start.ts, #160: a tile per factory engine, a pick inserts it wired; Esc or "Start from an empty canvas" is the bare patch). The Patches and Assemblies tabs are one Library tab (`#library`; `#presets` / `#assemblies` in the address still open it). The editor keeps a 40-step snapshot undo of the patch JSON for structural changes (Ctrl/Cmd+Z, Shift for redo). On the canvas a collapsed assembly is one card whose port dots carry the inner module ids, so dragging cables works unchanged; an expanded one (the card's expand / collapse button) is a frame laid out around its member cards, which get the expose / pin toggles, and dropping a card into or out of the frame changes membership. Once the cards have a size, an expansion pushes the cards and frames near the new frame out of its way (tidy.ts `clearAround`, `pushAside`), among the fellow members first and then one level up to the canvas, as part of its undo step ([D-644](ledger/editor.md#D-644), Proposed). The outermost assembly moves down and right so every open level's header fits on the canvas (`keepOnCanvas`): a frame clipped at the canvas's edge is drawn lower than it is.

Several rows at once (#186): in pick mode (the frame's `card · N`) and Arrange mode (the card menu's Arrange the card) the parameter rows can be selected (`Editor.rowSel`, `module:param` keys, kept per mode and cleared when the mode changes): a band from the empty canvas or a card's empty space, Shift-click a range, Cmd / Ctrl-click one row (inside the modes it only selects; outside, Cmd / Ctrl-click still shows or hides a row); Cmd / Ctrl+A selects every row, Esc clears the selection and a second Esc ends the mode. Jacks are never selected and stay one click each. Pick mode: Space / Enter or the bar's `show N` / `hide N` applies `pickRows` (any selected row hidden: all shown, else all hidden), a member card's header check box does the same for all its parameters (`pickState`: all / none / some), and `select all` / `select none` in the frame's bar act on every parameter at once. Arrange mode works on a tree of the card's rows (`web/src/arrange.ts`, tests `arrange.test.ts`): `layoutOf` reads the flat `ParamRef` list and `AssemblyInst.heads` (headings with no rows, each anchored to a row: before the section that row opens, or `after` the last row of that row's column) into columns › sections › rows, `pagesOf` cuts a section into pages as `groupStarts` does, the operations edit the tree and `setLayout` / `flatten` write it back (every factory card comes back unchanged). The editor draws the rows from the tree (`Editor.arrangeRows`) and, once they are laid out, an overlay (`.arr-ov`, `drawArrange`) over the card: a faint outline around each section (its heading and pages) and each page, the page's ⠿ handle in a strip on its outline's left, **+ heading** under each section and at each column's top (it stays when it has no rows; the FM-1 has no page for it), **+ page break** between two rows of a page, and the × of a break set by hand. The heading field shows the FM-1's name for its pages in mono, in parentheses (`fm1Name`). A drag has one landing line per place (`rowSpots` and the page and section spots, `nearestSpot`): a row lands at the start of a page (it opens the page: it takes over the page's flag), between two rows, at the end of a page, or into an empty section, never into another page that already has `PAGE_ROWS` knobs; a page's handle moves the page whole (between pages, a section's top or end, an empty section); a heading's grip moves its section (a column's top, between sections, a column's end; never above the rows a column goes on with). Every move first pins each page start (`pinPages`), so the pages around it stay as they were; a row that started a page hands the start to the next row. A selected row drags the selection as one block. Delete or the bar's `take off N` takes the selection off the card. Each of these is one undo step. On touch there is no band: a long press on a row starts a selection, taps add or remove rows, a selected row drags the block at once, a tap on the empty canvas ends it.

## Engines, voices, drums (#159, 2026-10-21)

The words the editor uses for assemblies, as the FM-1 community uses "engine" (the instrument a track plays):
